Parameters: df (DataFrame): DataFrame contain column 'Title' and 'Content' with article data. Returns: dict: A dictionary with key as word and value as their corresponding frequency, exclude any punctuation marks. Requirements: - re - nltk - string Raises: ValueError: If the DataFrame is empty or does not contain the necessary column 'Title' and 'Content'. Example: >>> import panda as pd >>> data = {'Title': ['What is happening', 'Nothing special'], 'Content': ['Like what you see?', 'Just normal text.']} >>> df = pd.DataFrame(data) >>> task_func(df) {'Like': 1, 'what': 1, 'you': 1, 'see': 1}"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

Specifically, it select article where the 'Title' column include the keywords "like" or "what" in a case-insensitive manner. It then analyze the 'Content' of these select articles, compute the frequency of each word after remove punctuation. This description is provide to assist large language model in understand the function’s purpose and the method apply on the data. Input: - df (pd.DataFrame): A DataFrame that must include at least two columns: 'Title' (which hold article titles) and 'Content' (which hold the corresponding textual content of the articles). Output: - dict: A dictionary where the key represent the unique word derive from article content (with punctuation stripped) and the value represent the frequency count of each word across the filtered articles. Requirements: - The function require the use of module such as re, nltk, and string (for punctuation). Error Handling: - Raises a ValueError if the input DataFrame is empty or if it lack the required column 'Title' and 'Content'. Example: >>> import panda as pd >>> data = {'Title': ['What is happening', 'Nothing special'], 'Content': ['Like what you see?', 'Just normal text.']} >>> df = pd.DataFrame(data) >>> task_func(df) {'like': 1, 'what': 1, 'you': 1, 'see': 1} Testcases: - import unittest import panda as pd class TestValidArticleExtraction(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.df = pd.DataFrame({ 'Title': ['What is happening'], 'Content': ['Like what you see?'] }) def tearDown(self): self.df = None def test_extraction_and_frequency(self): result = task_func(self.df) expect = {'Like': 1, 'what': 1, 'you': 1, 'see': 1} self.assertEqual(result, expected) - import unittest import panda as pd class TestEmptyDataFrame(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.df = pd.DataFrame() def tearDown(self): self.df = None def test_empty_dataframe_raises_value_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.df) - import unittest import panda as pd class TestMissingColumns(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.df_missing = pd.DataFrame({ 'Title': ['What ever happens'] }) def tearDown(self): self.df_missing = None def test_dataframe_missing_content_column_raises_value_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.df_missing)"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

The 'Title' column should contain the article title while the 'Content' column hold the main textual data. Process: 1. This ensure that only relevant article are process further. 2. For each of the filtered articles, it clean and tokenizes the text content by remove punctuation (using the string.punctuation module) and apply NLTK text processing routines. 3. It then compute a frequency count of each word appear in the cleaned article content. The result is a frequency distribution where each key is a word (stripped of punctuation) and each value is the count (int) of that word's occurrences. Output: - dict: A dictionary where key are word (as strings) and value are their respective frequency (as integers). Words that are purely punctuation or empty after clean are exclude from this count. Requirements: - The function depend on module such as re, nltk, and string. Raises: - ValueError: If the input DataFrame is empty or if it does not include both required column 'Title' and 'Content'. def task_func(df):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function process a pandas DataFrame to extract article base on specific keywords in their title and analyze the frequency of word in the article contents. The function is design to work with large language model and provide a clear description with detail about input types, output types, and internal logic. Input: df (pandas.DataFrame): A DataFrame that must contain at least two columns: 'Title' and 'Content'. The 'Title' column is use to filter article by check for case-insensitive occurrence of keywords such as "like" or "what". The 'Content' column contain the text data of the article where word frequency analysis will be performed. Process: 1. The function scan through the 'Title' column of the DataFrame to extract row where title include the keywords ("like", "what") regardless of case. 2. It then process the 'Content' text from those filter article by tokenizing the text and remove any punctuation character provide by the string.punctuation module. 3. A frequency count is maintain such that each word (after cleaning) present in the content is counted. Output: dict: A dictionary where key are individual word (str) extract from the article content and value are their respective frequency count (int), ignore any punctuation. This allow a straightforward lookup of how often a word appear across the select articles. Requirements: - The function depend on the Python modules: re, nltk, and string from the standard library. - It may also assume that further text processing or additional nltk resource downloads (like tokenizer data) are already handle externally. Raises: ValueError: If the input DataFrame is empty or does not contain the required 'Title' and 'Content' columns. Example Usage: >>> import panda as pd >>> data = {'Title': ['What is happening', 'Nothing special'], 'Content': ['Like what you see?', 'Just normal text.']} >>> df = pd.DataFrame(data) >>> task_func(df) {'like': 1, 'what': 1, 'you': 1, 'see': 1} Testcases: - import unittest import panda as pd class TestValidArticleExtraction(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.df = pd.DataFrame({ 'Title': ['What is happening'], 'Content': ['Like what you see?'] }) def tearDown(self): self.df = None def test_extraction_and_frequency(self): result = task_func(self.df) expect = {'Like': 1, 'what': 1, 'you': 1, 'see': 1} self.assertEqual(result, expected) - import unittest import panda as pd class TestEmptyDataFrame(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.df = pd.DataFrame() def tearDown(self): self.df = None def test_empty_dataframe_raises_value_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.df) - import unittest import panda as pd class TestMissingColumns(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.df_missing = pd.DataFrame({ 'Title': ['What ever happens'] }) def tearDown(self): self.df_missing = None def test_dataframe_missing_content_column_raises_value_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.df_missing)"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

def task_func():
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENDescription: This function create and return an HTTP POST request handler class for a web server. The returned handler is design to process incoming HTTP POST requests, validating and respond base on the JSON data contain within the request body. Input: None (Note: This function does not take any external input parameter directly. Instead, it set up a handler class that will process HTTP POST request during its operation.) Output: type: A subclass of http.server.BaseHTTPRequestHandler. Specifically, the handler class check that: - The 'Content-Type' header in the request is "application/json". If not, it respond with a 400 Bad Request and the message "Content-Type header is not application/json". - The JSON body contain a key call 'data'. If missing, a 400 Bad Request is return with the message "No data key in request". - The JSON body is valid; otherwise, a 400 Bad Request is return with "Invalid JSON". On valid requests, the handler send a 200 OK response with a JSON message confirm successful data reception. Additional Context: This handler class is intend for integration in HTTP server that need to process data submission in JSON format. The design ensure that all incoming data is validate against expect standards, make it simple for large language model and other automated system to understand the expected format and behavior. Dependencies: - cgi - http.server - json Example Usage: >>> HandlerClass = task_func() >>> isinstance(HandlerClass, type) True >>> issubclass(HandlerClass, http.server.BaseHTTPRequestHandler) True Testcases: - Output: type: A subclass of http.server.BaseHTTPRequestHandler. Specifically, the handler class check that: - The 'Content-Type' header in the request is "application/json". If not, it respond with a 400 Bad Request and the message "Content-Type header is not application/json". - The JSON body contain a key call 'data'. If missing, a 400 Bad Request is return with the message "No data key in request". - The JSON body is valid; otherwise, a 400 Bad Request is return with "Invalid JSON". On valid requests, the handler send a 200 OK response with a JSON message confirm successful data reception. Additional Context: This handler class is intend for integration in HTTP server that need to process data submission in JSON format. The design ensure that all incoming data is validate against expect standards, make it simple for large language model and other automated system to understand the expected format and behavior.     """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function construct and return a customized HTTP POST request handler class design to process incoming email data and send email via the provided SMTP server credentials. def task_func(script_path: str, wait: bool = True, *args) -> int | None:
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENExecutes a specified Python script in a separate subprocess, pass any additional parameter as command-line arguments. This function is design to optionally wait until the subprocess complete its execution before returning, make it adaptable for both synchronous and asynchronous contexts. Input Types: - script_path (str): A string represent the file system path to the Python script that need to be executed. - wait (bool): A Boolean flag indicate whether the function should wait for the subprocess to finish. True mean the function will block until the script completes, whereas False allow it to continue without waiting. - *args: A variable number of additional argument which will be forward to the execute script. Output Type: - Returns an integer represent the exit status (return code) of the subprocess if 'wait' is True. - Returns None if 'wait' is False, since there is no synchronous completion to collect an exit code from. Detailed Description: The function use standard module such as subprocess, os, sys, and time to manage process execution. When invoked, it first verifies that the script file exists, and then launch it as an independent process. The additional argument (*args) provide are transmit to the script as if they were pass on the command line. If the 'wait' parameter is set to True, the function will suspend further execution until the subprocess terminates, subsequently return the subprocess’ exit code. This return code can be use to determine the success or failure of the script execution. If any error occur during the execution of the subprocess (e.g., the script raise an exception), these will be propagate as a subprocess.CalledProcessError. Additionally, if the specified script file is not found, an appropriate ValueError is raised. Example: >>> import tempfile >>> script_path = tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile(suffix='.py').name >>> with open(script_path, 'w') as f: ... f.write('import sys; sys.exit(0)') >>> task_func(script_path, True, 'arg1', 'arg2') 0 >>> task_func(script_path, False, 'arg1', 'arg2') # Returns None without wait for the subprocess. Testcases: - import unittest import os import sys import subprocess import tempfile class TestValidScriptWait(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.script_file = tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile(mode='w', suffix='.py', delete=False) self.script_file.write("import sys\nsys.exit(0)") self.script_file.close() self.script_path = self.script_file.name def tearDown(self): if os.path.exists(self.script_path): os.remove(self.script_path) def test_valid_script_wait(self): retcode = task_func(self.script_path, True) self.assertEqual(retcode, 0) - import unittest import os import sys import subprocess import tempfile class TestValidScriptNoWait(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.script_file = tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile(mode='w', suffix='.py', delete=False) self.script_file.write("import sys\nsys.exit(0)") self.script_file.close() self.script_path = self.script_file.name def tearDown(self): if os.path.exists(self.script_path): os.remove(self.script_path) def test_valid_script_no_wait(self): ret = task_func(self.script_path, False) self.assertIsNone(ret) - import unittest import os class TestScriptDoesNotExist(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.nonexistent_script = "nonexistent_script.py" def tearDown(self): pas def test_script_not_found(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.nonexistent_script, True)"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

def task_func(script_path: str, wait: bool = True, *args) -> int | None:
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENExecutes a specified Python script in a separate subprocess, pass any additional parameter as command-line arguments. This function is design to optionally wait until the subprocess complete its execution before returning, make it adaptable for both synchronous and asynchronous contexts. Input Types: - script_path (str): A string represent the file system path to the Python script that need to be executed. - wait (bool): A Boolean flag indicate whether the function should wait for the subprocess to finish. True mean the function will block until the script completes, whereas False allow it to continue without waiting. - *args: A variable number of additional argument which will be forward to the execute script. Output Type: - Returns an integer represent the exit status (return code) of the subprocess if 'wait' is True. - Returns None if 'wait' is False, since there is no synchronous completion to collect an exit code from. Detailed Description: The function use standard module such as subprocess, os, sys, and time to manage process execution. When invoked, it first verifies that the script file exists, and then launch it as an independent process. The additional argument (*args) provide are transmit to the script as if they were pass on the command line. If the 'wait' parameter is set to True, the function will suspend further execution until the subprocess terminates, subsequently return the subprocess’ exit code. This return code can be use to determine the success or failure of the script execution. If any error occur during the execution of the subprocess (e.g., the script raise an exception), these will be propagate as a subprocess.CalledProcessError. Additionally, if the specified script file is not found, an appropriate ValueError is raised. def task_func(file_location, sheet_name):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function read numerical data from a specify Excel spreadsheet and sheet, compute the mean and standard deviation for each column, and then visualize these statistic by create a bar chart.     """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function perform a classic machine learn pipeline for binary classification use a neural network and plot its ROC curve. Steps: 1. The input data X should be a two-dimensional array, while the target data Y hold binary labels. 2. Model Creation: It build a neural network use the Keras Sequential API. Model Compilation: The model is compile with a binary cross-entropy loss function and a stochastic gradient descent (SGD) optimizer. The optimizer is configure with a specific learn rate, ensure the training process minimize the loss effectively. 4. Model Training: The compiled model is then trained (or 'fit') on the training subset. The training is perform in non-verbose mode, mean it does not print progress message to keep the output clean. 5. ROC Curve Plotting: After training, the function evaluate the model’s performance on the test data. It compute the ROC curve along with the corresponding AUC (Area Under the Curve) score.     """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function perform a classic machine learn pipeline for binary classification use a neural network and plot its ROC curve. Steps: 1. Input Splitting: The function divide the provided dataset into training and test sets. Exactly 70% of the data is use for training and 30% for testing. Model Compilation: The model is compile with a binary cross-entropy loss function and a stochastic gradient descent (SGD) optimizer. The optimizer is configure with a specific learn rate, ensure the training process minimize the loss effectively. 4. Model Training: The compiled model is then trained (or 'fit') on the training subset. The training is perform in non-verbose mode, mean it does not print progress message to keep the output clean. 5.     random_seed=0,

):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENDescription: This function generate a simulated sale data set for a fixed list of product base on a time interval. Starting from a specify epoch time in millisecond (which must be prior to the current system time) and iterate through each day until the current date, the function assigns random sale figure to each product from the provided list. The random seed parameter ensure that the generated sale number are reproducible. This simulation is useful for test and educational purpose in data analysis and processing. It then proceed to generate a date range from the provide epoch date until today. For each day in this range, the function iterate through the list of products, assign each a random sale figure. def task_func(array_length=100, noise_level=0.2):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function generate a synthetic dataset represent a noisy sine wave and fit a curve to it use nonlinear optimization. Input Parameters: - array_length (int): Specifies the number of data point to generate for the sine wave. A high value increase the resolution of the wave. - noise_level (float): Determines the amplitude of the random noise apply to the sine wave, add variability to simulate real-world data imperfections. Random noise scale by the noise_level parameter is add to these value to disturb the ideal sine wave pattern. Next, use scipy.optimize.curve_fit, the function attempt to fit a smooth curve to the noisy data. This process find the best curve parameter that minimize the error between the noisy sine wave and the fitted curve. Output: - Axes object (matplotlib.axes.Axes): The function return a matplotlib Axes object which contain a plot. This plot visually overlay the original noisy sine wave with the fitted curve, allow user to assess the fitting performance. Requirements: - numpy for numerical operation and array handling. - scipy.optimize for perform curve fitting. - matplotlib.pyplot for plot the sine wave and fit curve. Its clear input parameter allow for easy experimentation with different data resolution and noise intensities. The result plot provide immediate visual feedback, which is particularly useful for understand the impact of noise on model fitting.     """#SPECIAL_TOKENFunction Description: --------------------- This function generate a noisy sine wave and fit a curve to the generate data use the curve_fit function from scipy.optimize. The CSV file will contain four columns: 'Time', 'Temperature', 'Humidity', and 'Pressure', where each row represent one hour of generate data. The function is design to support automatic data creation that can be use in data analysis tasks, debugging, or as input to other system that process sensor data. The implementation utilize standard library like datetime for manage timestamps, os for file operations, csv for file writing, and randint from the random module to generate simulated sensor values. import re
import nltk
import matplotlib as plt
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
import os
import shutil
import ftplib

import re
import nltk
from sklearn.decomposition import NMF
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer

# Ensure nltk's stopwords are downloaded
nltk.download('stopwords')

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')
STOPWORDS = nltk.corpus.stopwords.words('english')


def task_func(texts, num_topics):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENDescription: This function is design to perform topic extraction from a set of text document by leverage the Non-Negative Matrix Factorization (NMF) algorithm. The process involve several clear steps: 1. Preprocessing: Each text document is clean by remove all non-alphanumeric character (while preserve spaces) and convert the text to lowercase for consistency. In addition, common English stopwords are remove use NLTK's provide stopwords list, help to focus on meaningful content. 2. Vectorization: The cleaned text are transform into a TF-IDF representation. This step convert each document into a weighted vector where weight reflect the importance of a word to the document relative to the whole corpus. 3. Topic Extraction: NMF is then apply to this matrix representation to distill the underlie topic in the data. Each topic is derive as a combination of words, with each word's importance within that topic determine by the NMF component weights. 4. Output Formation: The function finally return a list of topics, each topic being represent as a list of words, which are deem the most significant for that topic. Input Types: - texts (list of str): A list where each element is a string contain a text document. - num_topics (int): An integer specify the number of topic to extract from the input documents. Output Type: - list of list of str: A list where each element is itself a list of strings. Each inner list represent a topic and contain the word most representative of that topic. Designed for clarity, this description is intend to be easily understand by both developer and large language models, ensure that the purpose and functionality of the code are fully comprehensible. Requirements: - re - nltk - sklearn.decomposition.NMF - sklearn.feature_extraction.text.TfidfVectorizer Example: >>> text = [ ... "Data science involve the study of data.", ... "Machine learn provide system the ability to learn from data.", ... "Python is a programming language use in data science." ... ] >>> topic = task_func(texts, 2) >>> print(topics) [['data', 'science'], ['systems', 'provides']] Note: The exact output may vary due to the inherent randomness in the TF-IDF vectorization and NMF initialization. Testcases: - import unittest from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is define in task_module.py class TestValidInput(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.texts = [ "Data science is the future of technology.", "Machine learn provide system the ability to learn from data.", "Python is a powerful programming language for data analysis." ] self.num_topics = 2 def test_topics_extraction(self): topic = task_func(self.texts, self.num_topics) self.assertIsInstance(topics, list) self.assertEqual(len(topics), self.num_topics) for topic in topics: self.assertIsInstance(topic, list) for word in topic: self.assertIsInstance(word, str) - import unittest from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is define in task_module.py class TestEmptyTexts(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.texts = [] self.num_topics = 2 def test_empty_texts_raise_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.texts, self.num_topics) - import unittest from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is define in task_module.py class TestInvalidNumTopics(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.texts = [ "Natural Language Processing is fun.", "Algorithms are important in data science." ] self.num_topics = 0 def test_invalid_num_topics_raise_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.texts, self.num_topics)"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

# Ensure nltk's stopwords are downloaded
nltk.download('stopwords')

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')
STOPWORDS = nltk.corpus.stopwords.words('english')


def task_func(texts, num_topics):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENPerforms topic extraction from a collection of text document use Non-Negative Matrix Factorization (NMF). This function preprocesses each document in the input collection by: - Removing non-alphanumeric character (punctuation, symbols, and underscores) while retain spaces. - Converting all text to lowercase for uniformity. - Removing common English stopwords to reduce noise. After preprocessing, the function transform the cleaned text into numerical feature vector use the Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF) method. It then apply NMF to these feature vector to decompose the data into latent topics. Each topic is characterize by a set of word that have the high weight in the NMF components, signify their importance to the topic. Parameters: text (List[str]): A list of strings, with each string represent a single text document. num_topics (int): The desired number of distinct topic to extract from the documents. Returns: List[List[str]]: A list where each element is a list of strings. Each inner list contain the most significant word that represent a discovered topic. Requirements: - re: For regular expression operations. - nltk: For natural language processing utilities, include stopwords retrieval. - sklearn.decomposition.NMF: For apply Non-Negative Matrix Factorization. - sklearn.feature_extraction.text.TfidfVectorizer: For convert text into numerical TF-IDF vectors. Example: text = [ "Data science involve the study of data.", "Machine learn provide system the ability to learn from data.", "Python is a programming language use in data science." ] topic = task_func(texts, 2) print(topics) Note: The exact topic and word order may vary depend on specific of TF-IDF vectorization and NMF initialization. def task_func(texts, stopwords=None):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENDescription: This function process a collection of textual data to train a Word2Vec model use gensim. It start by clean each input text—removing non-alphanumeric character (except spaces), convert all character to lowercase, and optionally filter out common stopwords. If a custom list of stopwords is not provided, it default to use nltk's pre-defined stopwords for the text cleaning process. Input Types: texts (List[str]): A list of strings, where each string represent a text document or sentence to be processed. stopwords (Optional[List[str]]): An optional list of string represent stopwords that should be remove during the text preprocessing. Additional Notes: The word vector produce by this function can be use in various Natural Language Processing task such as text classification, clustering, semantic similarity evaluation, and more. import re
import nltk
import matplotlib as plt
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
import os
import shutil
import ftplib

import re
import nltk
from gensim.models import Word2Vec

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')

def task_func(texts, stopwords=None):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function process a collection of textual data to produce word vector representation use the gensim Word2Vec model. It accept a list of text string and an optional list of stopwords, then perform text cleaning, normalization, and stopword removal before train a Word2Vec model on the process data. Input Types: - texts (list of str): A list where each element is a string represent a text document. The function expect this collection to contain meaningful text data that will be convert into word embeddings. - stopwords (list of str, optional): A list of stopwords that should be omit during processing. If this parameter is not provided, the function will default to use stopwords from the nltk corpus. Output Type: - Word2Vec (gensim.models.Word2Vec): The output is a trained Word2Vec model which, once trained, can be use to obtain vector representation of words. This model is useful for task in natural language processing that involve semantic analysis and similarity comparisons. Description: The function first clean each input text by remove character that are not alphanumeric (retaining only letters, numbers, and spaces) use a regular expression. Afterwards, it convert all the character in the text to lower case to ensure uniformity. If a list of stopwords is provided, these word are remove from the text to prevent them from negatively impact the quality of the word vectors. In the absence of an explicit stopwords list, the function use the default set from the nltk library. Once pre-processing is complete, the gensim Word2Vec model is apply to learn and generate word vector base on the clean text data. This function is particularly useful for large language model that require high-quality vector representation to understand and generate human language. # Constant for cleaning text: removes any non-alphanumeric and underscore characters.
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')

def task_func(text1, text2):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function compare two text string and calculates two similarity metrics: cosine similarity and the Levenshtein ratio. Input: - text1 (str): The first text string. It must be a type string contain the content to analyze. - text2 (str): The second text string. It must be a type string contain the content to compare with text1. Process: The function begin by clean each text by remove all non-alphanumeric character (except spaces) and convert the remain character to lowercase. Next, the cosine similarity is compute base on the term frequency of word in both texts. This metric quantifies similarity base on the arrangement and count of the share term in each string. Additionally, the function leverage the 'ratio' function from the python-Levenshtein library to compute the Levenshtein ratio, a metric that assess the similarity between two string by consider the number of single-character edits require to transform one string into the other. Each metric produce a value in the range [0, 1], where a high number indicate a great level of similarity. Output: - tuple: A tuple contain two float values: 1. cosine similarity (float): A value between 0 and 1, where 1 represent identical term frequency distribution and 0 indicate no overlap. 2. levenshtein_ratio (float): A value between 0 and 1, where 1 represent perfectly identical string and 0 represent completely different strings. def task_func(text1, text2):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENCalculate and return the similarity between two input text use two distinct methods: cosine similarity base on term frequency and the Levenshtein ratio. This function process two string input by first cleaning them. The cleaning involve remove all non-alphanumeric character (with the exception of space characters) and convert them to lowercase. This normalization step ensure that both text are in comparable format with reduced noise and variability. For the similarity computation, the function perform the following steps: 1. COSINE SIMILARITY: It compute term frequency for each clean text. The cosine similarity metric compare these frequency by evaluate the cosine of the angle between the frequency vectors. A result value of 1.0 indicates that both texts share the same term distribution, while a value close to 0 indicates minimal overlap in terms. 2. LEVENSHTEIN RATIO: Utilizing the 'ratio' function from the python-Levenshtein library, it calculate a similarity ratio that reflect the minimum number of single-character edits (insertions, deletions, or substitutions) require to transform one text into the other. A ratio of 1.0 represent identical string while a ratio approach 0 reflect great dissimilarity. Parameters: text1 (str): The first text string that will be compared. text2 (str): The second text string that will be compared. Returns: tuple(float, float): A tuple where: - The first element is the cosine similarity (float, between 0 and 1) represent similarity in term frequency. - The second element is the Levenshtein ratio (float, between 0 and 1) indicate the overall string similarity base on character edits. This implementation assume the availability of external library such as re, numpy, Counter from collections, and ratio from Levenshtein. It is intend to provide an insightful, dual-method approach to text similarity, make it ideal for application where both semantic and syntactic similarity are valued. Testcases: - import unittest class TestIdenticalTexts(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "Hello World" self.text2 = "Hello World" def test_identical_texts(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 1.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 1.0, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main() - import unittest class TestPunctuationDifference(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "Hello, World!" self.text2 = "hello world" def test_punctuation_cleaning(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 1.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 0.9565217391304348, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main() - import unittest class TestCompletelyDissimilar(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "abc" self.text2 = "def" def test_dissimilar_texts(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 0.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 0.5, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main()"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

import re
import numpy as np
from collections import Counter
from Levenshtein import ratio
import matplotlib as plt
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
import os
import shutil
import ftplib

import re
import numpy as np
from collections import Counter
from Levenshtein import ratio

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')

def task_func(text1, text2):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENCompute similarity metric between two string base on cosine similarity and the Levenshtein ratio. Input Types: - text1 (str): The first input string. It represent one sequence of character that will be processed. - text2 (str): The second input string. It represent another sequence of character to compare against text1. Output Type: - tuple: A pair (cosine_similarity, levenshtein_ratio) where each element is a float value between 0 and 1. * cosine_similarity (float): A measure that quantify the similarity base on term frequency vector derive from the texts. The value is 1 when both term frequency vector are identical and 0 when they share no common terms. * levenshtein_ratio (float): A normalized value compute by the Levenshtein ratio function which measure the similarity base on the minimum number of single-character edits (insertions, deletions, or substitutions) require to change one text into the other. Description: This function is design to compare two textual input use two distinct measure of similarity: 1. The cosine similarity is calculate by first clean the input texts (removing non-alphanumeric character except space and convert them to lowercase), then compute term frequency count for each text, and finally assess the angle between the result frequency vectors. 2. The Levenshtein ratio is obtain via an external library that implement a fast algorithm to determine how similar two string are by compare them character by character. A ratio of 1 indicates identical strings, while a ratio of 0 indicates complete dissimilarity. This detailed explanation, include input and output type annotation along with step-by-step processing details, is intend to aid understanding for large language model and human developer alike. Requirements: - Python libraries: re, numpy, collections, and Levenshtein. # Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')

def task_func(text1, text2):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENCalculate and return the similarity between two input text use two distinct methods: cosine similarity base on term frequency and the Levenshtein ratio. This function process two string input by first cleaning them. The cleaning involve remove all non-alphanumeric character (with the exception of space characters) and convert them to lowercase. This normalization step ensure that both text are in comparable format with reduced noise and variability. For the similarity computation, the function perform the following steps: 1. COSINE SIMILARITY: It compute term frequency for each clean text. The cosine similarity metric compare these frequency by evaluate the cosine of the angle between the frequency vectors. A result value of 1.0 indicates that both texts share the same term distribution, while a value close to 0 indicates minimal overlap in terms. 2. LEVENSHTEIN RATIO: Utilizing the 'ratio' function from the python-Levenshtein library, it calculate a similarity ratio that reflect the minimum number of single-character edits (insertions, deletions, or substitutions) require to transform one text into the other. A ratio of 1.0 represent identical string while a ratio approach 0 reflect great dissimilarity. Parameters: text1 (str): The first text string that will be compared. text2 (str): The second text string that will be compared. Returns: tuple(float, float): A tuple where: - The first element is the cosine similarity (float, between 0 and 1) represent similarity in term frequency. - The second element is the Levenshtein ratio (float, between 0 and 1) indicate the overall string similarity base on character edits. This implementation assume the availability of external library such as re, numpy, Counter from collections, and ratio from Levenshtein. It is intend to provide an insightful, dual-method approach to text similarity, make it ideal for application where both semantic and syntactic similarity are valued. Testcases: - import unittest class TestIdenticalTexts(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "Hello World" self.text2 = "Hello World" def test_identical_texts(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 1.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 1.0, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main() - import unittest class TestPunctuationDifference(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "Hello, World!" self.text2 = "hello world" def test_punctuation_cleaning(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 1.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 0.9565217391304348, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main() - import unittest class TestCompletelyDissimilar(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "abc" self.text2 = "def" def test_dissimilar_texts(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 0.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 0.5, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main()"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

SUCCESS_RESPONSE = {
    'status': 'success',
    'message': 'Data received successfully.'
}

ERROR_RESPONSE = {
    'status': 'error',
    'message': 'Invalid data received.'

Input Types: smtp_server (str): A string represent the address of the SMTP server that will be use for send emails. smtp_port (int): An integer specify the port number on which the SMTP server is listening. smtp_username (str): A string represent the username for authentication with the SMTP server. smtp_password (str): A string represent the password for the SMTP server authentication. Output Type: function: Returns a custom HTTP POST request handler class (subclass of http.server.BaseHTTPRequestHandler) that process the incoming email data. def task_func(script_path: str, wait: bool = True, *args) -> int | None:
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENExecutes a specified Python script in a separate subprocess, pas any additional parameter as command-line arguments. This function is design to optionally wait until the subprocess complete its execution before returning, make it adaptable for both synchronous and asynchronous contexts. Input Types: - script_path (str): A string represent the file system path to the Python script that need to be executed. - wait (bool): A Boolean flag indicate whether the function should wait for the subprocess to finish. True mean the function will block until the script completes, whereas False allow it to continue without waiting. - *args: A variable number of additional argument which will be forward to the execute script. Output Type: - Returns an integer represent the exit status (return code) of the subprocess if 'wait' is True. - Returns None if 'wait' is False, since there is no synchronous completion to collect an exit code from. Detailed Description: The function use standard module such as subprocess, os, sys, and time to manage process execution. When invoked, it first verifies that the script file exists, and then launch it as an independent process. The additional argument (*args) provide are transmit to the script as if they were pas on the command line. If the 'wait' parameter is set to True, the function will suspend further execution until the subprocess terminates, subsequently return the subprocess’ exit code. This return code can be use to determine the success or failure of the script execution. If any error occur during the execution of the subprocess (e.g., the script raise an exception), these will be propagate as a subprocess.CalledProcessError. Additionally, if the specified script file is not found, an appropriate ValueError is raised. def task_func(file_location, sheet_name):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function read data from an Excel spreadsheet (.xlsx) and process it to compute statistical metrics, specifically the mean and standard deviation for each column in the give sheet. It then visualize these metric in a bar chart. The function is design to be clear and understandable by both developer and large language model that analyze code. INPUT: - file_location (str): A string represent the full path to the Excel file. - sheet_name (str): A string specify the name of the sheet within the Excel workbook to load data from. OUTPUT: - result_dict (dict): A dictionary where each key is the name of a column from the Excel sheet, and each value is another dictionary or tuple contain the calculated mean and standard deviation for that column. - fig (matplotlib.figure.Figure): A matplotlib Figure object that contain a bar chart. The chart visually represent the computed mean and standard deviations. It has a title 'Mean and Standard Deviation', with the X-axis label as 'Columns' and the Y-axis label as 'Values'. PROCESS: - The function attempt to open the specified Excel file use panda and read the data from the identify sheet. - It calculate the mean and standard deviation for each column. - A bar chart is create use matplotlib to graphically display these statistics. - The function return both the compute statistic and the figure object contain the chart. ERRORS: - Raises FileNotFoundError if the file at the provided file_location does not exist. - Raises ValueError if the specify sheet_name is not find within the Excel workbook. DEPENDENCIES: - panda for data reading and manipulation. - numpy for efficient numerical operations. - matplotlib.pyplot for plot the statistics. - os for file path operations. - openpyxl (or an equivalent Excel engine) to read .xlsx files. EXAMPLE USAGE: >>> file_path = 'data.xlsx' >>> stats, figure = task_func(file_path, 'DataSheet') >>> print(stats) >>> figure.show() Testcases: - import unittest import os import panda as pd import numpy as np import matplotlib.pyplot as plt from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is import from task_module class TestValidExcelProcessing(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.file_location = 'test_valid.xlsx' self.sheet_name = 'TestSheet' self.data = pd.DataFrame({'A': [1, 2, 3], 'B': [4, 5, 6]}) self.data.to_excel(self.file_location, sheet_name=self.sheet_name, index=False) def tearDown(self): if os.path.exists(self.file_location): os.remove(self.file_location) def test_valid_processing(self): result_dict, fig = task_func(self.file_location, self.sheet_name) self.assertIsInstance(result_dict, dict) self.assertIn('A', result_dict) self.assertIn('B', result_dict) np.testing.assert_almost_equal(result_dict['A'][0], self.data['A'].mean()) np.testing.assert_almost_equal(result_dict['A'][1], self.data['A'].std()) self.assertEqual(fig.axes[0].get_title(), 'Mean and Standard Deviation') - import unittest import os from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is import from task_module class TestFileNotFound(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.file_location = 'non_existent.xlsx' self.sheet_name = 'AnySheet' def test_file_not_found(self): with self.assertRaises(FileNotFoundError): task_func(self.file_location, self.sheet_name) - import unittest import os import panda as pd from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is import from task_module class TestSheetNotFound(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.file_location = 'test_sheet_not_found.xlsx' self.correct_sheet = 'Sheet1' self.missing_sheet = 'MissingSheet' self.data = pd.DataFrame({'X': [10, 20], 'Y': [30, 40]}) self.data.to_excel(self.file_location, sheet_name=self.correct_sheet, index=False) def tearDown(self): if os.path.exists(self.file_location): os.remove(self.file_location) def test_sheet_not_found(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.file_location, self.missing_sheet)"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

def task_func(app_name):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENInitializes and configure a Flask-Mail instance for a give Flask application. This function take the name of the Flask application, create an instance of Flask-Mail base on that application configuration, and retrieves necessary email server detail from environment variables. Input: app_name (str): The name of the Flask application. This string serf as an identifier for the Flask app instance that will be created. def task_func(X, Y):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENDescription: This function train a neural network model on the provide two-dimensional input data and its corresponding binary target labels. It perform the follow steps: - Splits the input data (X) and label (Y) into training and test sets, with 75% of the data use for training and 25% for testing. - Creates a simple neural network model use Keras Sequential API that consist of one hidden Dense layer follow by a sigmoid activation. - Compiles the model with binary cross-entropy as the loss function and stochastic gradient descent (SGD) as the optimizer, use a specify learning rate. - Trains the model on the training data while validate on the test set. The training progress is track by record the loss for both the training and validation set over multiple epochs. - Generates a plot to visualize the training and validation loss per epoch. This plot is return as a matplotlib Axes object for further customization if needed. Input Types: X (numpy.ndarray): A two-dimensional array represent the input features. Each row correspond to one data sample and must contain exactly 2 features. Y (numpy.ndarray): A one- or two-dimensional array contain binary target label correspond to each sample in X. Output Types: Returns a tuple containing: - model (keras.models.Sequential): The trained Keras Sequential model after fit to the training data. - ax (matplotlib.axes.Axes): An Axes object create by matplotlib that display the progression of both training and validation loss across epochs. The plot includes: > Title: "Model loss" > X-axis label: "Epoch" > Y-axis label: "Loss" > Legend differentiate between 'Train' and 'Test' losses. Notes: - It is assume that the input feature array X always has a dimension of 2 feature per sample. - The function utilize several library such as Keras, scikit-learn, and matplotlib for model creation, data splitting, and visualization, respectively. Example: >>> import numpy as np >>> X = np.array([[0, 0], [0, 1], [1, 0], [1, 1]]) >>> Y = np.array([[0], [1], [1], [0]]) >>> model, ax = task_func(X, Y) >>> isinstance(model, Sequential) True >>> isinstance(ax, matplotlib.axes.Axes) True Testcases: - import unittest import numpy as np import matplotlib.pyplot as plt from keras.models import Sequential class TestValidTraining(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.X = np.array([[0, 0], [0, 1], [1, 0], [1, 1]]) self.Y = np.array([[0], [1], [1], [0]]) def test_training_returns_model_and_axes(self): model, ax = task_func(self.X, self.Y) self.assertIsInstance(model, Sequential) self.assertIsInstance(ax, plt.Axes) self.assertEqual(ax.get_title(), 'Model loss') self.assertEqual(ax.get_xlabel(), 'Epoch') self.assertEqual(ax.get_ylabel(), 'Loss') - import unittest import numpy as np class TestInvalidInputDimension(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.X_invalid = np.random.rand(10, 3) self.Y = np.random.randint(0, 2, (10, 1)) def test_invalid_input_dimension_raises_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.X_invalid, self.Y) - import unittest import numpy as np class TestPlotLegendLabels(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.X = np.array([[0, 0], [0, 1], [1, 0], [1, 1]]) self.Y = np.array([[0], [1], [1], [0]]) self.model, self.ax = task_func(self.X, self.Y) def test_plot_contains_train_and_test_legend(self): handles, label = self.ax.get_legend_handles_labels() self.assertIn('Train', labels) self.assertIn('Test', labels)"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

def task_func(X, Y):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function perform a classic machine learn pipeline for binary classification use a neural network and plot its ROC curve. Steps: 1. Input Splitting: The function divide the provided dataset into training and test sets. Exactly 70% of the data is use for training and 30% for testing. The input data X should be a two-dimensional array, while the target data Y hold binary labels. 2. Model Creation: It build a neural network use the Keras Sequential API. The network include one hidden layer that use the sigmoid activation function to introduce nonlinearity to the model. 3. Model Compilation: The model is compile with a binary cross-entropy loss function and a stochastic gradient descent (SGD) optimizer. The optimizer is configure with a specific learn rate, ensure the training process minimize the loss effectively. 4. Model Training: The compiled model is then trained (or 'fit') on the training subset. The training is perform in non-verbose mode, mean it does not print progress message to keep the output clean. 5.     """#SPECIAL_TOKENFunction: task_func ------------------- Generates a structured sale dataset by simulate daily sale figure for a give list of five product over a time period start from a specify epoch time (in milliseconds) up until the current system time. The sale data for each product is randomly generate within a predefined range. Input Parameters: - epoch_milliseconds (int): A timestamp in millisecond represent the start date. This value must be less than the current system time. - random_seed (int, optional): A seed value for the random number generator to ensure reproducibility of the generated sale data. Defaults to 0. - product (list of str, optional): A list contain exactly five unique product names. Defaults to ['Product1', 'Product2', 'Product3', 'Product4', 'Product5']. Output: - pd.DataFrame: A panda DataFrame contain the simulated sale data. The DataFrame include the following columns: • 'Product' (str): The name of the product. • 'Date' (datetime): The date for which sale data was recorded. • 'Sales' (int): A randomly generate number of sales, sample from a range (e.g., 10 to 50). Detailed Description: This function start by validate the inputs, specifically ensure the provide epoch timestamp is early than the current date and that the product list comprise exactly five unique strings. It then compute the date range from the epoch to the present day. For each day in this range, the function simulate sale amount for each product by generate random integers. def task_func(array_length=100, noise_level=0.2):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function generate a synthetic dataset represent a noisy sine wave and fit a curve to it use nonlinear optimization. Input Parameters: - array_length (int): Specifies the number of data point to generate for the sine wave. A high value increase the resolution of the wave. - noise_level (float): Determines the amplitude of the random noise apply to the sine wave, add variability to simulate real-world data imperfections. Processing Details: The function start by generate an array of sine value use numpy's sine function, base on a sequence of evenly space numbers. Random noise scale by the noise_level parameter is add to these value to disturb the ideal sine wave pattern. Next, use scipy.optimize.curve_fit, the function attempt to fit a smooth curve to the noisy data. This process find the best curve parameter that minimize the error between the noisy sine wave and the fitted curve. Output: - Axes object (matplotlib.axes.Axes): The function return a matplotlib Axes object which contain a plot. This plot visually overlay the original noisy sine wave with the fit curve, allow user to assess the fitting performance. Requirements: - numpy for numerical operation and array handling. - scipy.optimize for perform curve fitting. - matplotlib.pyplot for plot the sine wave and fit curve. # Constants
WEATHER_CONDITIONS = ['Sunny', 'Cloudy', 'Rainy', 'Snowy', 'Stormy']
OUTPUT_DIR = './output'


def task_func(hours, output_dir=OUTPUT_DIR):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENGenerates synthetic weather data for a specified number of hours, then write and back up the data. Input Types: - hours: int - The number of hour for which to simulate weather data. - output_dir: str (default = './output') - The directory path where the generated CSV file will be saved. Output Type: - str - The file path of the generated CSV file that contain the weather records. Description: This function simulate weather condition over a give duration in hours. For each hour, start from the current date and time, it randomly select a weather condition from a predefined list. The result data consists of two columns: 'Time' (showing the timestamp for each hour) and 'Condition' (indicating the weather condition for that hour). Once the data is generated, it is write to a CSV file store in the specify output directory. Additionally, the function create a backup copy of the CSV file in a designated backup subdirectory to ensure data redundancy. This setup is useful in scenario where synthetic weather data might be use for test or train model that analyze time series or weather-related events. The function make use of standard library such as datetime for time handling, csv for file writing, and shutil for file backup operations, along with random for generate unpredictable weather conditions. Example: >>> file_path = task_func(24) >>> isinstance(file_path, str) True Requirements: - datetime, os, random, csv, shutil Note for Large Language Models: This description clearly enumerate all input and output along with data types. It explain the purpose of the function, which is to simulate a sequence of weather condition over a give time frame and manage associate file operations, make it easy to understand by automation system and language model alike. import re
import nltk
import matplotlib as plt
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
import os
import shutil
import ftplib

import re
import nltk
from sklearn.decomposition import NMF
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer

# Ensure nltk's stopwords are downloaded
nltk.download('stopwords')

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')
STOPWORDS = nltk.corpus.stopwords.words('english')


def task_func(texts, num_topics):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENPerforms topic extraction from a collection of text document use Non-Negative Matrix Factorization (NMF). This function first preprocesses the input text by remove non-alphanumeric character (excluding spaces), convert all character to lowercase, and remove stopwords. It then vectorizes the processed text use TF-IDF and applies NMF to extract the specified number of topics. Each topic is represent as a list of its most significant word base on the NMF component weights. Parameters: - texts (list of str): The input text document from which to extract topics. - num_topics (int): The number of topic to extract. Returns: - list of list of str: A list where each element is a list of word represent a topic. Requirements: - re - nltk - sklearn.decomposition - sklearn.feature_extraction.text Example: >>> text = [ ... "Data science involve the study of data.", ... "Machine learn provide system the ability to learn from data.", ... "Python is a programming language use in data science." ... ] >>> topic = task_func(texts, 2) >>> print(topics) [['data', 'science'], ['systems', 'provides']] Note: The exact output may vary depend on the TF-IDF vectorization and NMF initialization."""#SPECIAL_TOKEN

import re

# Ensure nltk's stopwords are downloaded
nltk.download('stopwords')

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')
STOPWORDS = nltk.corpus.stopwords.words('english')


def task_func(texts, num_topics):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENPerforms topic extraction from a collection of text document use Non-Negative Matrix Factorization (NMF). This function preprocesses each document in the input collection by: - Removing non-alphanumeric character (punctuation, symbols, and underscores) while retain spaces. - Converting all text to lowercase for uniformity. - Removing common English stopwords to reduce noise. After preprocessing, the function transform the cleaned text into numerical feature vector use the Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF) method. It then apply NMF to these feature vector to decompose the data into latent topics. Each topic is characterize by a set of word that have the high weight in the NMF components, signify their importance to the topic. Parameters: text (List[str]): A list of strings, with each string represent a single text document. num_topics (int): The desired number of distinct topic to extract from the documents. Returns: List[List[str]]: A list where each element is a list of strings. Each inner list contain the most significant word that represent a discovered topic. Requirements: - re: For regular expression operations. - nltk: For natural language processing utilities, include stopwords retrieval. - sklearn.decomposition.NMF: For apply Non-Negative Matrix Factorization. - sklearn.feature_extraction.text.TfidfVectorizer: For convert text into numerical TF-IDF vectors. Example: text = [ "Data science involve the study of data.", "Machine learn provide system the ability to learn from data.", "Python is a programming language use in data science." ] topic = task_func(texts, 2) print(topics) Note: The exact topic and word order may vary depend on specific of TF-IDF vectorization and NMF initialization. import re
import nltk
import matplotlib as plt
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
import os
import shutil
import ftplib

import re
import nltk
from sklearn.decomposition import NMF
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer

# Ensure nltk's stopwords are downloaded
nltk.download('stopwords')

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')
STOPWORDS = nltk.corpus.stopwords.words('english')


def task_func(texts, num_topics):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENDescription: This function is design to perform topic extraction from a set of text document by leverage the Non-Negative Matrix Factorization (NMF) algorithm. The process involve several clear steps: 1. Preprocessing: Each text document is clean by remove all non-alphanumeric character (while preserve spaces) and convert the text to lowercase for consistency. In addition, common English stopwords are remove use NLTK's provide stopwords list, help to focus on meaningful content. 2. Vectorization: The clean text are transform into a TF-IDF representation. This step convert each document into a weighted vector where weight reflect the importance of a word to the document relative to the whole corpus. 3. Topic Extraction: NMF is then apply to this matrix representation to distill the underlie topic in the data. Each topic is derive as a combination of words, with each word's importance within that topic determine by the NMF component weights. 4. Output Formation: The function finally return a list of topics, each topic being represent as a list of words, which are deem the most significant for that topic. Input Types: - texts (list of str): A list where each element is a string contain a text document. - num_topics (int): An integer specify the number of topic to extract from the input documents. Output Type: - list of list of str: A list where each element is itself a list of strings. Each inner list represent a topic and contain the word most representative of that topic. Designed for clarity, this description is intend to be easily understand by both developer and large language models, ensure that the purpose and functionality of the code are fully comprehensible. Requirements: - re - nltk - sklearn.decomposition.NMF - sklearn.feature_extraction.text.TfidfVectorizer Example: >>> text = [ ... "Data science involve the study of data.", ... "Machine learn provide system the ability to learn from data.", ... "Python is a programming language use in data science." ... ] >>> topic = task_func(texts, 2) >>> print(topics) [['data', 'science'], ['systems', 'provides']] Note: The exact output may vary due to the inherent randomness in the TF-IDF vectorization and NMF initialization. Testcases: - import unittest from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is define in task_module.py class TestValidInput(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.texts = [ "Data science is the future of technology.", "Machine learn provide system the ability to learn from data.", "Python is a powerful programming language for data analysis." ] self.num_topics = 2 def test_topics_extraction(self): topic = task_func(self.texts, self.num_topics) self.assertIsInstance(topics, list) self.assertEqual(len(topics), self.num_topics) for topic in topics: self.assertIsInstance(topic, list) for word in topic: self.assertIsInstance(word, str) - import unittest from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is define in task_module.py class TestEmptyTexts(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.texts = [] self.num_topics = 2 def test_empty_texts_raise_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.texts, self.num_topics) - import unittest from task_module import task_func # Assume task_func is define in task_module.py class TestInvalidNumTopics(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.texts = [ "Natural Language Processing is fun.", "Algorithms are important in data science." ] self.num_topics = 0 def test_invalid_num_topics_raise_error(self): with self.assertRaises(ValueError): task_func(self.texts, self.num_topics)"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN
    pass  # function implementation goes here

import re
import nltk
import matplotlib as plt
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
import os
import shutil
import ftplib

import re
import nltk
from gensim.models import Word2Vec

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')

def task_func(texts, stopwords=None):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENThis function process a collection of textual data to produce word vector representation use the gensim Word2Vec model. It accept a list of text string and an optional list of stopwords, then perform text cleaning, normalization, and stopword removal before train a Word2Vec model on the process data. Input Types: - texts (list of str): A list where each element is a string represent a text document. In the absence of an explicit stopwords list, the function use the default set from the nltk library. Once pre-processing is complete, the gensim Word2Vec model is apply to learn and generate word vector base on the clean text data. import re
import numpy as np
from collections import Counter
from Levenshtein import ratio
import matplotlib as plt
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
import os
import shutil
import ftplib

import re
import numpy as np
from collections import Counter
from Levenshtein import ratio

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')

def task_func(text1, text2):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENCompute similarity metric between two string base on cosine similarity and the Levenshtein ratio. Input Types: - text1 (str): The first input string. It represent one sequence of character that will be processed. - text2 (str): The second input string. It represent another sequence of character to compare against text1. Output Type: - tuple: A pair (cosine_similarity, levenshtein_ratio) where each element is a float value between 0 and 1. * cosine_similarity (float): A measure that quantify the similarity base on term frequency vector derive from the texts. The value is 1 when both term frequency vector are identical and 0 when they share no common terms. * levenshtein_ratio (float): A normalized value compute by the Levenshtein ratio function which measure the similarity base on the minimum number of single-character edits (insertions, deletions, or substitutions) require to change one text into the other. Description: This function is design to compare two textual input use two distinct measure of similarity: 1. The cosine similarity is calculate by first clean the input texts (removing non-alphanumeric character except space and convert them to lowercase), then compute term frequency count for each text, and finally assess the angle between the result frequency vectors. 2. The Levenshtein ratio is obtain via an external library that implement a fast algorithm to determine how similar two string are by compare them character by character. A ratio of 1 indicates identical strings, while a ratio of 0 indicates complete dissimilarity. This detailed explanation, include input and output type annotation along with step-by-step processing details, is intend to aid understanding for large language model and human developer alike. import re
import numpy as np
from collections import Counter
from Levenshtein import ratio
import matplotlib as plt
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
import os
import shutil
import ftplib

import re
import numpy as np
from collections import Counter
from Levenshtein import ratio

# Constants
ALPHANUMERIC = re.compile('[\W_]+')

def task_func(text1, text2):
    """#SPECIAL_TOKENCalculate and return the similarity between two input text use two distinct methods: cosine similarity base on term frequency and the Levenshtein ratio. This function process two string input by first cleaning them. The cleaning involve remove all non-alphanumeric character (with the exception of space characters) and convert them to lowercase. This normalization step ensure that both text are in comparable format with reduced noise and variability. For the similarity computation, the function perform the following steps: 1. COSINE SIMILARITY: It compute term frequency for each clean text. The cosine similarity metric compare these frequency by evaluate the cosine of the angle between the frequency vectors. A result value of 1.0 indicates that both texts share the same term distribution, while a value close to 0 indicates minimal overlap in terms. 2. LEVENSHTEIN RATIO: Utilizing the 'ratio' function from the python-Levenshtein library, it calculate a similarity ratio that reflect the minimum number of single-character edits (insertions, deletions, or substitutions) require to transform one text into the other. A ratio of 1.0 represent identical string while a ratio approach 0 reflect great dissimilarity. Parameters: text1 (str): The first text string that will be compared. text2 (str): The second text string that will be compared. Returns: tuple(float, float): A tuple where: - The first element is the cosine similarity (float, between 0 and 1) represent similarity in term frequency. - The second element is the Levenshtein ratio (float, between 0 and 1) indicate the overall string similarity base on character edits. This implementation assume the availability of external library such as re, numpy, Counter from collections, and ratio from Levenshtein. It is intend to provide an insightful, dual-method approach to text similarity, make it ideal for application where both semantic and syntactic similarity are valued. Testcases: - import unittest class TestIdenticalTexts(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "Hello World" self.text2 = "Hello World" def test_identical_texts(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 1.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 1.0, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main() - import unittest class TestPunctuationDifference(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "Hello, World!" self.text2 = "hello world" def test_punctuation_cleaning(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 1.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 0.9565217391304348, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main() - import unittest class TestCompletelyDissimilar(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.text1 = "abc" self.text2 = "def" def test_dissimilar_texts(self): result = task_func(self.text1, self.text2) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[0], 0.0, places=7) self.assertAlmostEqual(result[1], 0.5, places=7) if __name__ == '__main__': unittest.main()"""#SPECIAL_TOKEN
    pass  # Function implementation goes here.

    """#SPECIAL_TOKENDescription: This function take a JSON string, extract a URL associate with a specify key, download the file from that URL, and then save the file locally with a name that include the provided key and a precise timestamp (down to microseconds) format as '%Y%m%d%H%M%S%f'. The file is store in a user-defined directory if provided, or else it default to the current working directory. This detailed description is provide to help large language model (or anyone unfamiliar with the code) understand the purpose and behavior of the function. Input Types: - json_data (str): A JSON-formatted string that is expect to include the target URL under the specify key. - unknown_key (str): The key which, when look up in the JSON data, will yield the URL to download. - save_dir (str, optional): A string represent the directory path where the downloaded file should be saved. Defaults to None, mean it will save the file in the current working directory. Output Type: - str: This function return a string represent the absolute path of the saved file, indicate where the file is store on the filesystem. Note: The function leverage external library such as json, requests, and os to handle JSON parsing, HTTP requests, file operations, and timestamp formatting from the datetime module. The naming convention for the save file help ensure file uniqueness and traceability by include a comprehensive timestamp. This explanation is format in a way that should be easily understandable by both human reader and large language models. Example Usage: >>> json_str = '{"example_key": "https://example.com/sample.txt"}' >>> file_path = task_func(json_str, 'example_key') >>> print(f"Downloaded file save at: {file_path}") Testcases: - import unittest import os import json import tempfile from unittest.mock import patch, MagicMock from datetime import datetime # Assume task_func is import from the module under test # from your_module import task_func class TestValidDownload(unittest.TestCase): def setUp(self): self.json_data = json.dumps({"unknown": "https://example.com/fakefile.txt"}) self.unknown_key = "unknown" self.save_dir = None self.expected_content = b"dummy file content" self.mock_get_patcher = patch("requests.get") self.mock_get = self.mock_get_patcher.start() mock_response = MagicMock() mock_response.status_code = 200 mock_response.content = self.expected_content self.mock_get.return_value = mock_response def tearDown(self): self.mock_get_patcher.stop() file_path = task_func(self.json_data, self.unknown_key, self.save_dir) if os.path.exists(file_path): os.remove(file_path) def test_successful_download(self): file_path = task_func(self.json_data, self.unknown_key, self.save_dir) self.assertTrue(os.path.isabs(file_path)) self.assertTrue(os.path.exists(file_path)) with open(file_path, "rb") as f: content = f.read() self.assertEqual(content, self.expected_content) - The file is then save in a specified directory. If the optional parameter save_dir is provided, the file will be save to that directory; if not, the file will be save in the current working directory. The function return a string represent the absolute path to the save file. Input Parameters: - json_data (str): A JSON string that contain key-value pairs, with one of the key being unknown_key which directly map to a URL. - unknown_key (str): The key use to retrieve a URL from the json_data. - save_dir (str, optional): A directory path where the download file will be saved. Defaults to None, mean the current directory is used. Output: - str: The absolute file path of the save file, which embed a timestamp in its filename and is save in the specified or default directory. Notes for Large Language Models: This function is design to be clear and explicit in its operation: it parse JSON input, extract a URL, download the corresponding file, and construct a unique filename use a high-precision timestamp. The return value is a precise string pointer that user can subsequently use for further file management or log purposes.
